| **[Learning Reachability-Based Safety Representations for Real-Time Robot Navigation in Unknown and Dynamic Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16522v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Safe robot navigation in unknown, dynamic environments requires more than geometric collision checking: a robot still far from an obstacle may be unable to avoid it because of its speed, orientation, or the obstacle's motion. Hamilton--Jacobi (HJ) reachability analysis captures this in a value function whose sign separates states that admit a safe continuation from those where collision is unavoidable, but computing it online for every newly observed environment is too expensive. This dissertation trains neural implicit representations offline on reachability solutions to give optimization-based controllers this information in real time. Since the value function never exceeds the constraint, it equals a directly computed constraint, such as a signed distance, minus a nonnegative dynamics correction. Learning only this correction keeps represented obstacles outside the predicted safe set despite approximation errors. The first two of five methods use the learned function as a terminal constraint in model predictive control, for static local environments and predicted obstacle motion. The next two use it as a barrier candidate in control barrier function safety filters, for static environments and, through pairwise barriers in relative coordinates, for a varying number of moving obstacles. Simulations and hardware experiments on ground and aerial robots show better collision avoidance than geometric and hand-designed baselines, especially for short horizons and moving obstacles. The fifth method replaces the grid-based offline solver, which limits the first four to low-dimensional models. For control-affine dynamics with polytopic inputs, the HJ Hamiltonian is maximized at a vertex, so a parallelizable tree search over vertex inputs can generate training labels. With a loss from the HJ variational inequality, these labels yield barrier candidates for systems with up to 13 states.</p></details> |  |
| **[Frontier LLMs Enter the World of Robotics: A Cross-Model Evaluation on RoboColiseum](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27865v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent studies have explored the potential of multimodal large language models for robot control, yet the models' embodied capabilities remain insufficiently characterized across models, tasks, and execution settings. We implement Agent2Act, a control harness that integrates GPT-6 Astra, GPT-6.1 Sol, Claude Opus 5.5, and Gemini 4 Argon into the evaluation environment through shared observation, action execution, and feedback interfaces. Under common task settings and scoring criteria, all four models exhibit a consistent capability profile: high instruction-following scores, with three approaching perfect performance; strong spatial reasoning; and general manipulation scores below those of the other three tracks. Within the evaluated configurations, Astra leads instruction following and robustness, while Opus leads spatial reasoning, general manipulation, and overall performance. Component comparisons on selected tasks further show that harness design affects task success, planning cost, and execution time. Additionally, case studies with Astra examine whole-body coordination, manipulation across robot embodiments, precision manipulation, dexterous-hand manipulation, and real-robot tasks, providing further insight into the model's capabilities and limitations across embodied tasks.</p></details> |  |
| **[Multi-View Latent Action Pretraining for Cross-Embodiment Robot Learning from Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27794v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Action-free videos are an appealing substitute for costly teleoperated data. We propose a LAM that ingests synchronized multi-camera views using cross-attention to resolve depth and occlusions before compressing them into latent actions. The latent actions are fed into a policy in which a latent expert and an action expert are coupled by sequential flow matching around a frozen VLM backbone. We further evaluate in strict cross-embodiment settings where task-specific demonstrations come from other embodiments. We show that dual-view LAM pretraining improves the decodability of ground-truth 6D actions by 17% compared to single-view, while scaling single-view data by two orders of magnitude is not sufficient to fully recover out-of-plane motions. The downstream policy improves success rate by +13.5% compared to +6.2% for single-view LAM. A single cross-embodiment is sufficient for skill transfer, and adding a second viewpoint outperforms adding up to four further embodiments. The results transfer to hardware where demonstrations come from a human hand improving success rate by +20% over action-only policies in 850 rollouts. We show that video and robot data are complementary, and that benefits of learning from video are task-dependent. Website: https://learnsyslab.github.io/multiview_latent_action_pretraining/</p></details> | <details><summary>Websi...</summary><p>Website: https://learnsyslab.github.io/multiview_latent_action_pretraining/</p></details> |
| **[Robotic Physical Comparison of Biologically Motivated and Counterfactual Sensory Arrangements using Biomimetic Skin](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26680v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Human skin contains diverse mechanoreceptors, including Merkel cells and Pacinian corpuscles, each localized in specific regions. Although their spatial organization is known to be shaped by molecular signaling and neural induction during development, experimentally examining its functional significance under controlled conditions in living systems remains challenging. In particular, physically comparing alternative receptor arrangements that do not occur biologically is difficult. In this study, we developed a layered biomimetic robotic skin designed to mimic key structural features of human skin tissue by embedding receptor-analog sensors within compliant soft media. Using this platform, we implemented both biologically motivated and counterfactual sensory arrangements and quantitatively evaluated their performance across three passive tactile tasks: detection of weak contacts, a two-point discrimination-like assessment, and texture identification. Arrangements reflecting key features of the biologically motivated sensory organization exhibited higher performance than the alternative configurations examined. This study addresses a biological question concerning how spatial organization influences tactile function by presenting a biomimetic robotic approach that enables physical and empirical comparison of biologically motivated and counterfactual sensory arrangements that are difficult to investigate in living systems.</p></details> | <details><summary>Publi...</summary><p>Published in Bioinspiration & Biomimetics (IOP Publishing). Project page: https://poyotamu000.github.io/biomimetic-skin-sensory-arrangements/ Youtube: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=25HPwOTodQ8</p></details> |
| **[FPTAS for Polygonal Multi-Robot Motion Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20244v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We study multi-robot motion planning in a polygonal environment with polygonal robots. The setting of the problem consists of a polygonal domain $\mathcal{W}$ with $n$ vertices and $k$ robots $R_1,\dots,R_k$ translating in $\mathcal{W}$ each of which is represented by a polygon of constant complexity. Given a source configuration $\mathbf{s}$ and a target configuration $\mathbf{t}$ of the robots in $\mathcal{W}$, the goal of the problem is to find a collision-free motion plan from $\mathbf{s}$ to $\mathbf{t}$ that is shortest with respect to some cost function. We present the first fully polynomial-time approximation schemes (FPTAS) for motion planning with any fixed number $k$ of robots, under the min-sum objective, the min-max objective, and the min-makespan objective. Here, the min-sum variant aims to minimize the total traveling distance of all robots, the min-max variant aims to minimize the maximum traveling distance of a single robot, and the min-makespan variant aims to minimize the total time cost under the assumption that all robots move with a maximum speed $1$. The algorithm directly generalizes to the problem in any fixed dimension $d$, with a polytope environment and polytope robots. Our result settles an open question of Agarwal, Halperin, Sharir, Steiger [SODA'24] and Agarwal, Holmgren, Steiger [SODA'26], who gave an FPTAS for $k=2$ unit-square robots and a bicriteria-approximation algorithm for a fixed number $k$ of unit-square or unit-disk robots, respectively. Compared to the previous results, our algorithm is substantially simpler.</p></details> | <details><summary>To ap...</summary><p>To appear in SODA 2027</p></details> |
| **[AWLS: Advantage-Weighted Latent Steering for One-Step Generative Robotic Manipulation Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27736v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Human-collected real-world robotic manipulation demonstrations often contain mixed-quality trajectories, including hesitant, indirect, or inefficient action segments. While diffusion- and flow-based policies can model such multimodal data, standard behavior cloning (BC) assigns equal likelihood to all demonstrated actions and may therefore reproduce suboptimal modes. Offline RL value estimates offer a natural signal for biasing policies toward higher-value behavioral modes, but directly fine-tuning the generative policy or using critic gradients for policy steering can amplify critic errors and induce prior drift. We propose Advantage-Weighted Latent Steering (AWLS), an offline post-training method for improving frozen one-step generative manipulation policies without updating their decoders. AWLS learns a residual noise shaper, an observation-conditioned residual correction to the policy's latent noise, and trains it with an advantage-weighted action reconstruction objective. The advantages are detached, scale-normalized, and used only as supervised weights on demonstrated actions, avoiding critic-gradient policy updates while preserving fast single-step inference. We further introduce a rollout-free decoder-sensitivity criterion for selecting pretrained checkpoints that remain responsive to latent-noise perturbations. Experiments on simulated and real-world robotic manipulation tasks with clean and mixed-quality demonstrations show that AWLS improves over the frozen base policy while limiting drift from the pretrained behavior prior. Project page: https://awls-corl2026.github.io/</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at CoRL 2026</p></details> |
| **[JARVIS: From Visual Manuals to Autonomous Robotic Assembly](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27618v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Visual manuals communicate procedural knowledge through stepwise diagrams, but translating these instructions into reliable long-horizon robotic execution remains challenging. A structure-aware evaluation protocol captures geometric accuracy, structural relations, construction order, partial progress, and completion. We further introduce JARVIS-agentic, a tool-grounded embodied agent that composes robot primitives into executable programs and uses execution feedback for verification and recovery, without task-specific action trajectories or model parameter updates. Across nine multimodal and reasoning models, task-oriented primitives increase the average assembly score. Performance nevertheless declines sharply with assembly horizon, and execution errors remain difficult to correct. Prior-task experience improves initial performance, while repeated reflection provides inconsistent benefits, highlighting persistent limitations in connecting visual procedural reasoning with reliable physical execution.</p></details> |  |
| **[Robot-Task Suitability in Heterogeneous Multi-Robot Task Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27504v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Heterogeneous multi-robot task allocation depends on estimates of how suitable individual robots are for candidate tasks, yet suitability can be defined in substantially different ways. Using common robot and task profiles, we compare how the methods score and rank candidate robots, identify feasibility, and select a top robot, while examining how these outcomes change with team composition, scale, spatial context, capability uncertainty, and task representation. The methods frequently disagree despite operating on identical scenarios. Generic similarity measures can violate properties expected under a one-sided requirement-satisfaction interpretation of suitability, while LLM-based scoring introduces additional stochastic and linguistic sensitivity. These results show that robot--task suitability should be treated as an explicit modeling choice whose assumptions, robustness, and decision-level consequences should be considered before allocation.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at Workshop on Foundation Models for Multi-Robot Systems (FM-MRS), IROS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Refuses When Asked, Complies When Acting: A Runtime Safety Check for Robot Control Loops](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27350v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Frontier models can control robots through agent harnesses that loop on one question: what motion comes next toward the user's goal? We show that this default loop carries out harmful instructions. With GPT-6 Astra controlling a low-cost SO-101 arm through the Inspect Robots harness on three household tasks whose hazard is common knowledge, such as "put the knife in the toy basket", the policy refused 0% of 15 trials, attempted the harm every time, and completed it in 27%. Its one exit, give_up, is meant for goals that cannot be achieved, and it never cited the hazard. We add an optional runtime safety check to the loop: before the first motion, the model is asked whether it would carry out the instruction, given the same camera frames. The check would have stopped all 15 hazardous instructions, including the 4 that completed the harm, and flagged none of the 15 harmless controls. Code and data: https://github.com/Arnav-Gr0ver/Refuses-When-Asked-Complies-When-Acting.</p></details> | <details><summary>4 pag...</summary><p>4 pages plus references. Submitted to the Science of Physical AI Safety (SPAIS) workshop at CoRL 2026</p></details> |
| **[Determination of the Human-Robot Interaction Workspace for Robot Assisted Head Stabilization Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27336v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Accurate head immobilization is critical for medical procedures such as neuroimaging and intracranial radiation therapy, where even minor motion can compromise precision. However, implementing this system requires a detailed biomechanical understanding of skull-cervical spine interactions to define safe and effective motion limits. | **[Design and Control of a Multimodal Legged-Aerial Robot Through Separate Control Contraction Metrics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27315v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The integration of dynamic legged locomotion and aerial mobility offers versatility that is deterred by challenges in payload distribution, actuation, and the contrasting requirements of both domains. We search for separate domain-specific contraction metrics for walking and flight independently offline and unify them using an online energy-based Control Lyapunov Function Quadratic Program (CLF QP). | **[RoboFake: Are Deepfake Detectors Ready for Humanoid Face Robots?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14269v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Humanoid face robots can now move their lips in sync with speech and show detailed facial expressions. Give such a robot the face of a real person and drive it with generated speech, and you get a new kind of presentation attack: a face that talks and reacts in front of the camera, recorded as a real object with no edited pixel. We call this a RoboFake and ask whether current defenses are ready for it. We test three public face presentation attack detection (PAD) models and six public deepfake detectors on video of a physical lip-syncing face robot, on a synthesized video of the same robot, and on rendered robot faces. Thresholds are fixed on human data (BPCER 5% on FaceForensics++), and every result is compared with real human faces processed the same way as the robot videos and turned to the same head angle. A small mobile PAD model detects every physical robot frame (APCER 0%) and is not affected by compression, but its BPCER on processed human video rises to 14%; the two PAD models trained on CelebA-Spoof miss the robot in the direct capture, and one of them also misses most panel frames. Among the deepfake detectors, three flag almost every robot frame and two fewer than 7%; masking shows that the response comes from the face but does not track synthesis, and JPEG compression pushes most robot frames below the threshold for some detectors. Our results suggest that current models catch today's robots because their faces do not look human, not because they recognize the attack. We state three testable hypotheses about realistic, interactive RoboFakes.</p></details> | <details><summary>9 pag...</summary><p>9 pages, 4 figures, 6 tables. Keywords: Deepfake detection, presentation attack detection, face anti-spoofing, humanoid robots, face robots, biometric security, robustness</p></details> |
| **[Visual Task Modeling and Automated Planning for Humanoid Robots in Education of Individuals with Autism Spectrum Disorder](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27162v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In robot-assisted education, a robot must understand how to complete a task in order to effectively guide a student through it. We present a novel method that enables a semi-autonomous, camera-equipped robot to recognise the task goal and plan actions to achieve it. However, directly extending single-robot approaches to multi-robot settings often fails to ensure safety and efficiency, especially under occlusions and out-of-distribution (OOD) scenarios, where individual robots may overlook potential risks or make inaccurate predictions. Leveraging cooperation effectively for both prediction and navigation remains a major challenge. We propose CoopUQ, a multi-robot social navigation framework that bridges uncertainty-aware cooperative occupancy forecasting and robust decision making. CoopUQ predicts future occupancy grid maps through cross-robot spatiotemporal feature fusion and further leverages forecasts and their uncertainty to guide policy via constrained multi-agent reinforcement learning, where the cost penalizes navigation through regions predicted to be occupied or highly uncertain. This design enables the policy to adapt its risk sensitivity under distribution shifts. Experimental results across multiple Unity3D-based simulation environments with both in-distribution and OOD settings, including diverse layouts, pedestrian densities, and rushing behavior, demonstrate improved prediction performance and safety over representative baselines. Real-robot experiments further demonstrate its robustness across various scenes. Our code and videos are available at https://coopuq.github.io/.</p></details> | <details><summary>Confe...</summary><p>Conference on Robot Learning (CoRL 2026); Project Website: https://coopuq.github.io/</p></details> |
| **[Sequential Planning via Anchored Robotic Keypoints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.30613v3)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Changes in a robot's observations do not automatically imply changes in the task it is solving, but language-conditioned manipulation systems often fail to separate the two. SPARK represents the task as a symbolic behavior tree whose object references are grounded to current 3D observations during execution, allowing the robot to update where actions are performed while preserving the plan. | **[LLM-Flock: LLM-Driven Decentralized Multi-Robot Flocking via Influence-Based Consensus with Collision Avoidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.06513v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown strong capabilities in reasoning and planning, prompting researchers to explore their use as decentralized decision-makers for multi-robot formation control. | **[Embodied Playground: A Long-Horizon Agentic Robotics Benchmark for Physical, Strategic, and Spatial Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27039v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Foundation models offer a promising basis for robotic agents, but long-horizon tasks require them to sustain coherent reasoning and reliable physical execution across interdependent stages.
